========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… I suppose what brings me in is that I’m frightened of what happens when I start eating. It’s not just, you know, having a bit more than I meant to. It feels like something gets away from me. Once I start, my hands just keep going back for more, and I’m sitting there thinking, Tom, stop this now, you’ve had enough, just stop — but I don’t. It’s like the thought is there, plain as day, but it doesn’t seem to reach my hands somehow.

And I know that probably sounds strange, especially at my age, but it’s been scaring me. I don’t feel in charge in those moments, and that’s the part that made me think I’d better talk to someone rather than just keep pretending I can handle it on my own.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’ve tried to work that out, actually, because I knew if I came in here and just said “a lot,” that wouldn’t help much. I started writing it down in a little notebook—what I ate, when it happened, and what I did afterward—and looking back over it, it’s been going on for many months now.

Nearly every week there’s been at least one afternoon where it gets out of hand. Afternoons seem to be the worst for me. I’ll start eating and then it just keeps going, and afterward I’ll panic and try to make up for it somehow—sometimes by making myself sick, sometimes laxatives, sometimes not eating for a while after, or pushing myself too hard with walking around or doing chores.

So… not every day, no, but it’s not rare either. When I look at the logs, it’s almost every week, at least once, and it’s been that way for quite a while.

I’ll start eating and then it just keeps going, and afterward I’ll panic and try to make up for it somehow—sometimes by making myself sick, sometimes laxatives, sometimes not eating for a while after, or pushing myself too hard with walking around or doing chores.\n\nSo… not every day, no, but it’s not rare either. When I look at the logs, it’s almost every week, at least once, and it’s been that way for quite a while.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you worry about gaining weight as a result of these eating episodes?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 78 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Fear_of_Weight_Gain\nmanifestation: \"Even with my doctor saying I’m too thin, if the scale is up half a pound I feel panicky and ashamed, like I’ve let myself go and have to fix it right away.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… very much. More than I like to admit, really. It sounds foolish when I say it out loud, because my doctor has told me I’m too thin, and I do hear that, I do understand the words. But it doesn’t feel that way in my head.

If I get on the scale and it’s up even half a pound, I feel this rush of panic, like I’ve done something wrong. I’ll stand there staring at the number as if it’s proof that I’ve let myself go, even if it’s such a tiny change that anyone else would probably say it means nothing. I feel ashamed, almost dirty with myself, and then I start thinking I have to fix it right away. I can’t just leave it alone and say, “Well, that’s normal,” or “It’s just a fluctuation.” It grabs hold of me.

So yes, after those eating episodes especially, I’m terrified I’ll gain weight from it. It’s not just a passing worry. It feels urgent, like I’ve lost control and need to undo the damage somehow.

I started writing it down in a little notebook—what I ate, when it happened, and what I did afterward—and looking back over it, it’s been going on for many months now.\n\nNearly every week there’s been at least one afternoon where it gets out of hand. Afternoons seem to be the worst for me. I’ll start eating and then it just keeps going, and afterward I’ll panic and try to make up for it somehow—sometimes by making myself sick, sometimes laxatives, sometimes not eating for a while after, or pushing myself too hard with walking around or doing chores.\n\nSo… not every day, no, but it’s not rare either. When I look at the logs, it’s almost every week, at least once, and it’s been that way for quite a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight as a result of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… very much. More than I like to admit, really. It sounds foolish when I say it out loud, because my doctor has told me I’m too thin, and I do hear that, I do understand the words. But it doesn’t feel that way in my head.\n\nIf I get on the scale and it’s up even half a pound, I feel this rush of panic, like I’ve done something wrong. I’ll stand there staring at the number as if it’s proof that I’ve let myself go, even if it’s such a tiny change that anyone else would probably say it means nothing. I feel ashamed, almost dirty with myself, and then I start thinking I have to fix it right away. I can’t just leave it alone and say, “Well, that’s normal,” or “It’s just a fluctuation.” It grabs hold of me.\n\nSo yes, after those eating episodes especially, I’m terrified I’ll gain weight from it. It’s not just a passing worry. It feels urgent, like I’ve lost control and need to undo the damage somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others tell you that you're too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I know that probably doesn’t make sense to other people, because my doctor has said straight out that I’m too thin. And I hear it, I really do. I’m not trying to be difficult. But when I look at myself, or when I get on the scale, it doesn’t feel true in my head.\n\nIf the number is up even half a pound, I get this awful panicky feeling, like I’ve suddenly let myself go. I feel ashamed, like I’ve done something wrong and I have to fix it right away. Yes… that is the main reason, I think. I wish I could say it was something else, or that it was just because I felt physically uncomfortable, but really it’s because I get frightened that the food is going to stay in me and turn into weight.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brings me in is that I’m frightened of what happens when I start eating. It’s not just, you know, having a bit more than I meant to. It feels like something gets away from me. Once I start, my hands just keep going back for more, and I’m sitting there thinking, Tom, stop this now, you’ve had enough, just stop — but I don’t. It’s like the thought is there, plain as day, but it doesn’t seem to reach my hands somehow.\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds strange, especially at my age, but it’s been scaring me. I don’t feel in charge in those moments, and that’s the part that made me think I’d better talk to someone rather than just keep pretending I can handle it on my own.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I’m ashamed to say it, but there are times after I’ve eaten too much where I go straight to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. It’s not every single bite or anything like that, but when I feel I’ve gone too far, I get this awful panic that the food is just sitting there inside me, and I can’t settle.\n\nAnd I’ve taken laxatives too, for the same reason, really. I get scared the food will stay in me, and I tell myself I’m just trying to undo it somehow. I know that probably sounds terrible, especially saying it out loud, but yes… I have done that.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating too much happen?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to work that out, actually, because I knew if I came in here and just said “a lot,” that wouldn’t help much. I started writing it down in a little notebook—what I ate, when it happened, and what I did afterward—and looking back over it, it’s been going on for many months now.\n\nNearly every week there’s been at least one afternoon where it gets out of hand. Afternoons seem to be the worst for me. I’ll start eating and then it just keeps going, and afterward I’ll panic and try to make up for it somehow—sometimes by making myself sick, sometimes laxatives, sometimes not eating for a while after, or pushing myself too hard with walking around or doing chores.\n\nSo… not every day, no, but it’s not rare either. When I look at the logs, it’s almost every week, at least once, and it’s been that way for quite a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight as a result of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… very much. More than I like to admit, really. It sounds foolish when I say it out loud, because my doctor has told me I’m too thin, and I do hear that, I do understand the words. But it doesn’t feel that way in my head.\n\nIf I get on the scale and it’s up even half a pound, I feel this rush of panic, like I’ve done something wrong. I’ll stand there staring at the number as if it’s proof that I’ve let myself go, even if it’s such a tiny change that anyone else would probably say it means nothing. I feel ashamed, almost dirty with myself, and then I start thinking I have to fix it right away. I can’t just leave it alone and say, “Well, that’s normal,” or “It’s just a fluctuation.” It grabs hold of me.\n\nSo yes, after those eating episodes especially, I’m terrified I’ll gain weight from it. It’s not just a passing worry. It feels urgent, like I’ve lost control and need to undo the damage somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others tell you that you're too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I know that probably doesn’t make sense to other people, because my doctor has said straight out that I’m too thin. And I hear it, I really do. I’m not trying to be difficult. But when I look at myself, or when I get on the scale, it doesn’t feel true in my head.\n\nIf the number is up even half a pound, I get this awful panicky feeling, like I’ve suddenly let myself go. I feel ashamed, like I’ve done something wrong and I have to fix it right away. It’s not like I can calmly tell myself, “That’s nothing, that’s just normal.” I know other people might see it that way, but I don’t feel it that way. It feels much bigger than it is.\n\nDoctor: Is your main reason for making yourself vomit or using laxatives after eating too much because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… that is the main reason, I think. I wish I could say it was something else, or that it was just because I felt physically uncomfortable, but really it’s because I get frightened that the food is going to stay in me and turn into weight.\n\nThere are times after I’ve eaten too much where I’ll go straight to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. I don’t feel calm about it at all—it’s more like panic, like I have to get rid of it before it “counts,” if that makes sense. And I’ve taken laxatives for the same reason, because I’m scared of the food sitting inside me. It sounds awful saying it plainly, but yes… it’s mostly the fear of gaining weight that makes me do it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think it is, though I don’t like admitting that. It feels shameful, especially at my age, to say that a number on a scale or how I think my body looks can decide whether I feel all right with myself or not. But it does.

Even when my doctor tells me I’m too thin, that doesn’t really settle it inside me. If I step on the scale and it’s up even half a pound, I can feel myself change right away. I get panicky, and ashamed, like I’ve let myself go somehow. It’s ridiculous, maybe, because half a pound shouldn’t mean anything, but to me it feels like I’ve failed, like I need to fix it immediately.

So yes, my weight and shape have too much power over how I feel about myself. More than anything else sometimes. It’s like I can’t judge myself fairly unless that number feels “safe,” and if it doesn’t, I feel awful about who I am.

It’s not every single bite or anything like that, but when I feel I’ve gone too far, I get this awful panic that the food is just sitting there inside me, and I can’t settle.\n\nAnd I’ve taken laxatives too, for the same reason, really. I get scared the food will stay in me, and I tell myself I’m just trying to undo it somehow. I know that probably sounds terrible, especially saying it out loud, but yes… I have done that.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating too much happen?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to work that out, actually, because I knew if I came in here and just said “a lot,” that wouldn’t help much. I started writing it down in a little notebook—what I ate, when it happened, and what I did afterward—and looking back over it, it’s been going on for many months now.\n\nNearly every week there’s been at least one afternoon where it gets out of hand. Afternoons seem to be the worst for me. I’ll start eating and then it just keeps going, and afterward I’ll panic and try to make up for it somehow—sometimes by making myself sick, sometimes laxatives, sometimes not eating for a while after, or pushing myself too hard with walking around or doing chores.\n\nSo… not every day, no, but it’s not rare either. When I look at the logs, it’s almost every week, at least once, and it’s been that way for quite a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight as a result of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… very much. More than I like to admit, really. It sounds foolish when I say it out loud, because my doctor has told me I’m too thin, and I do hear that, I do understand the words. But it doesn’t feel that way in my head.\n\nIf I get on the scale and it’s up even half a pound, I feel this rush of panic, like I’ve done something wrong. I’ll stand there staring at the number as if it’s proof that I’ve let myself go, even if it’s such a tiny change that anyone else would probably say it means nothing. I feel ashamed, almost dirty with myself, and then I start thinking I have to fix it right away. I can’t just leave it alone and say, “Well, that’s normal,” or “It’s just a fluctuation.” It grabs hold of me.\n\nSo yes, after those eating episodes especially, I’m terrified I’ll gain weight from it. It’s not just a passing worry. It feels urgent, like I’ve lost control and need to undo the damage somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others tell you that you're too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I know that probably doesn’t make sense to other people, because my doctor has said straight out that I’m too thin. And I hear it, I really do. I’m not trying to be difficult. But when I look at myself, or when I get on the scale, it doesn’t feel true in my head.\n\nIf the number is up even half a pound, I get this awful panicky feeling, like I’ve suddenly let myself go. I feel ashamed, like I’ve done something wrong and I have to fix it right away. It’s not like I can calmly tell myself, “That’s nothing, that’s just normal.” I know other people might see it that way, but I don’t feel it that way. It feels much bigger than it is.\n\nDoctor: Is your main reason for making yourself vomit or using laxatives after eating too much because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… that is the main reason, I think. I wish I could say it was something else, or that it was just because I felt physically uncomfortable, but really it’s because I get frightened that the food is going to stay in me and turn into weight.\n\nThere are times after I’ve eaten too much where I’ll go straight to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. I don’t feel calm about it at all—it’s more like panic, like I have to get rid of it before it “counts,” if that makes sense. And I’ve taken laxatives for the same reason, because I’m scared of the food sitting inside me. It sounds awful saying it plainly, but yes… it’s mostly the fear of gaining weight that makes me do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the most important thing when it comes to how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, though I don’t like admitting that. It feels shameful, especially at my age, to say that a number on a scale or how I think my body looks can decide whether I feel all right with myself or not. But it does.\n\nEven when my doctor tells me I’m too thin, that doesn’t really settle it inside me. If I step on the scale and it’s up even half a pound, I can feel myself change right away. I get panicky, and ashamed, like I’ve let myself go somehow. It’s ridiculous, maybe, because half a pound shouldn’t mean anything, but to me it feels like I’ve failed, like I need to fix it immediately.\n\nSo yes, my weight and shape have too much power over how I feel about myself. More than anything else sometimes. It’s like I can’t judge myself fairly unless that number feels “safe,” and if it doesn’t, I feel awful about who I am.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I know that probably sounds terrible, especially saying it out loud, but yes… I have done that.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating too much happen?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to work that out, actually, because I knew if I came in here and just said “a lot,” that wouldn’t help much. I started writing it down in a little notebook—what I ate, when it happened, and what I did afterward—and looking back over it, it’s been going on for many months now.\n\nNearly every week there’s been at least one afternoon where it gets out of hand. Afternoons seem to be the worst for me. I’ll start eating and then it just keeps going, and afterward I’ll panic and try to make up for it somehow—sometimes by making myself sick, sometimes laxatives, sometimes not eating for a while after, or pushing myself too hard with walking around or doing chores.\n\nSo… not every day, no, but it’s not rare either. When I look at the logs, it’s almost every week, at least once, and it’s been that way for quite a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight as a result of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… very much. More than I like to admit, really. It sounds foolish when I say it out loud, because my doctor has told me I’m too thin, and I do hear that, I do understand the words. But it doesn’t feel that way in my head.\n\nIf I get on the scale and it’s up even half a pound, I feel this rush of panic, like I’ve done something wrong. I’ll stand there staring at the number as if it’s proof that I’ve let myself go, even if it’s such a tiny change that anyone else would probably say it means nothing. I feel ashamed, almost dirty with myself, and then I start thinking I have to fix it right away. I can’t just leave it alone and say, “Well, that’s normal,” or “It’s just a fluctuation.” It grabs hold of me.\n\nSo yes, after those eating episodes especially, I’m terrified I’ll gain weight from it. It’s not just a passing worry. It feels urgent, like I’ve lost control and need to undo the damage somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others tell you that you're too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I know that probably doesn’t make sense to other people, because my doctor has said straight out that I’m too thin. And I hear it, I really do. I’m not trying to be difficult. But when I look at myself, or when I get on the scale, it doesn’t feel true in my head.\n\nIf the number is up even half a pound, I get this awful panicky feeling, like I’ve suddenly let myself go. I feel ashamed, like I’ve done something wrong and I have to fix it right away. It’s not like I can calmly tell myself, “That’s nothing, that’s just normal.” I know other people might see it that way, but I don’t feel it that way. It feels much bigger than it is.\n\nDoctor: Is your main reason for making yourself vomit or using laxatives after eating too much because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… that is the main reason, I think. I wish I could say it was something else, or that it was just because I felt physically uncomfortable, but really it’s because I get frightened that the food is going to stay in me and turn into weight.\n\nThere are times after I’ve eaten too much where I’ll go straight to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. I don’t feel calm about it at all—it’s more like panic, like I have to get rid of it before it “counts,” if that makes sense. And I’ve taken laxatives for the same reason, because I’m scared of the food sitting inside me. It sounds awful saying it plainly, but yes… it’s mostly the fear of gaining weight that makes me do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the most important thing when it comes to how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, though I don’t like admitting that. It feels shameful, especially at my age, to say that a number on a scale or how I think my body looks can decide whether I feel all right with myself or not. But it does.\n\nEven when my doctor tells me I’m too thin, that doesn’t really settle it inside me. If I step on the scale and it’s up even half a pound, I can feel myself change right away. I get panicky, and ashamed, like I’ve let myself go somehow. It’s ridiculous, maybe, because half a pound shouldn’t mean anything, but to me it feels like I’ve failed, like I need to fix it immediately.\n\nSo yes, my weight and shape have too much power over how I feel about myself. More than anything else sometimes. It’s like I can’t judge myself fairly unless that number feels “safe,” and if it doesn’t, I feel awful about who I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your self-worth is largely defined by your weight and body shape?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s fair to say. I don’t like that it’s true, but it is. Even when my doctor tells me I’m too thin, it doesn’t seem to sink in the way it should. I can hear the words, but then I get on the scale and if it’s up even half a pound, everything in me just drops.\n\nI feel panicky, like something has gone terribly wrong, and ashamed too, like I’ve let myself go or lost control somehow. And then it’s not just about the number anymore — it feels like it says something about me, like I’ve failed and I have to fix it right away. I know half a pound probably sounds like nothing, but in my head it feels much bigger than that. So yes… my weight and shape do end up deciding how I feel about myself, more than I wish they did.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

It’s not every single time I eat too much, but… it has been happening regularly. I’ve been keeping food logs, and when I look back at them, for many months now, almost every week has at least one afternoon where I ate out of control and then tried to make up for it somehow.

It’s not just, you know, having a bit more than I meant to. It feels like something gets away from me. Once I start, my hands just keep going back for more, and I’m sitting there thinking, Tom, stop this now, you’ve had enough, just stop — but I don’t. It’s like the thought is there, plain as day, but it doesn’t seem to reach my hands somehow.\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds strange, especially at my age, but it’s been scaring me. I don’t feel in charge in those moments, and that’s the part that made me think I’d better talk to someone rather than just keep pretending I can handle it on my own.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I’m ashamed to say it, but there are times after I’ve eaten too much where I go straight to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. It’s not every single bite or anything like that, but when I feel I’ve gone too far, I get this awful panic that the food is just sitting there inside me, and I can’t settle.\n\nAnd I’ve taken laxatives too, for the same reason, really. I get scared the food will stay in me, and I tell myself I’m just trying to undo it somehow. I know that probably sounds terrible, especially saying it out loud, but yes… I have done that.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating too much happen?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to work that out, actually, because I knew if I came in here and just said “a lot,” that wouldn’t help much. I started writing it down in a little notebook—what I ate, when it happened, and what I did afterward—and looking back over it, it’s been going on for many months now.\n\nNearly every week there’s been at least one afternoon where it gets out of hand. Afternoons seem to be the worst for me. I’ll start eating and then it just keeps going, and afterward I’ll panic and try to make up for it somehow—sometimes by making myself sick, sometimes laxatives, sometimes not eating for a while after, or pushing myself too hard with walking around or doing chores.\n\nSo… not every day, no, but it’s not rare either. When I look at the logs, it’s almost every week, at least once, and it’s been that way for quite a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight as a result of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… very much. More than I like to admit, really. It sounds foolish when I say it out loud, because my doctor has told me I’m too thin, and I do hear that, I do understand the words. But it doesn’t feel that way in my head.\n\nIf I get on the scale and it’s up even half a pound, I feel this rush of panic, like I’ve done something wrong. I’ll stand there staring at the number as if it’s proof that I’ve let myself go, even if it’s such a tiny change that anyone else would probably say it means nothing. I feel ashamed, almost dirty with myself, and then I start thinking I have to fix it right away. I can’t just leave it alone and say, “Well, that’s normal,” or “It’s just a fluctuation.” It grabs hold of me.\n\nSo yes, after those eating episodes especially, I’m terrified I’ll gain weight from it. It’s not just a passing worry. It feels urgent, like I’ve lost control and need to undo the damage somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others tell you that you're too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I know that probably doesn’t make sense to other people, because my doctor has said straight out that I’m too thin. And I hear it, I really do. I’m not trying to be difficult. But when I look at myself, or when I get on the scale, it doesn’t feel true in my head.\n\nIf the number is up even half a pound, I get this awful panicky feeling, like I’ve suddenly let myself go. I feel ashamed, like I’ve done something wrong and I have to fix it right away. It’s not like I can calmly tell myself, “That’s nothing, that’s just normal.” I know other people might see it that way, but I don’t feel it that way. It feels much bigger than it is.\n\nDoctor: Is your main reason for making yourself vomit or using laxatives after eating too much because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… that is the main reason, I think. I wish I could say it was something else, or that it was just because I felt physically uncomfortable, but really it’s because I get frightened that the food is going to stay in me and turn into weight.\n\nThere are times after I’ve eaten too much where I’ll go straight to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. I don’t feel calm about it at all—it’s more like panic, like I have to get rid of it before it “counts,” if that makes sense. And I’ve taken laxatives for the same reason, because I’m scared of the food sitting inside me. It sounds awful saying it plainly, but yes… it’s mostly the fear of gaining weight that makes me do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the most important thing when it comes to how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, though I don’t like admitting that. It feels shameful, especially at my age, to say that a number on a scale or how I think my body looks can decide whether I feel all right with myself or not. But it does.\n\nEven when my doctor tells me I’m too thin, that doesn’t really settle it inside me. If I step on the scale and it’s up even half a pound, I can feel myself change right away. I get panicky, and ashamed, like I’ve let myself go somehow. It’s ridiculous, maybe, because half a pound shouldn’t mean anything, but to me it feels like I’ve failed, like I need to fix it immediately.\n\nSo yes, my weight and shape have too much power over how I feel about myself. More than anything else sometimes. It’s like I can’t judge myself fairly unless that number feels “safe,” and if it doesn’t, I feel awful about who I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your self-worth is largely defined by your weight and body shape?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s fair to say. I don’t like that it’s true, but it is. Even when my doctor tells me I’m too thin, it doesn’t seem to sink in the way it should. I can hear the words, but then I get on the scale and if it’s up even half a pound, everything in me just drops.\n\nI feel panicky, like something has gone terribly wrong, and ashamed too, like I’ve let myself go or lost control somehow. And then it’s not just about the number anymore — it feels like it says something about me, like I’ve failed and I have to fix it right away. I know half a pound probably sounds like nothing, but in my head it feels much bigger than that. So yes… my weight and shape do end up deciding how I feel about myself, more than I wish they did.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: It’s not every single time I eat too much, but… it has been happening regularly. I’ve been keeping food logs, and when I look back at them, for many months now, almost every week has at least one afternoon where I ate out of control and then tried to make up for it somehow.\n\nSometimes that meant making myself vomit. Other times I used laxatives, or I wouldn’t eat afterward, or I’d push myself with walking and chores until I felt like I’d done something to undo it. But vomiting is part of that pattern, yes. It’s usually after those afternoons when I feel like I’ve lost control, and then I panic and feel like I have to do something right away.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I do. I mean, not every time in exactly the same way, but after those times when I feel I’ve eaten too much, I usually feel this awful panic, like I can’t just let it sit there in me.

There are times I go to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. I hate saying that out loud, but that is what happens. It’s like I tell myself I’ll feel calmer afterward, or that I’ve undone some of the damage, even though I know that probably sounds strange.

And I’ve taken laxatives too, because I get scared the food will stay in me, or that I’ll gain from it. It’s not even always thought through clearly — it’s more like fear takes over and I’m trying to get rid of it somehow. So yes, I do try to make up for it that way.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think it is. I wish I could say it was about feeling sick or uncomfortable after eating, but if I’m being honest, the fear of gaining weight is what really sets it off.

Even when my doctor tells me I’m too thin, that doesn’t reassure me the way it probably should. I’ll hear it, and part of me knows I’m supposed to take that seriously, but then if I step on the scale and it’s up even half a pound, I just… I panic. It feels like I’ve let myself go, like I’ve lost control, and I feel ashamed, almost like I’ve done something wrong.

And once that feeling starts, it’s very hard to sit with it. My mind goes straight to, “I have to fix this right now.” So yes, the vomiting or laxatives are tied to that fear — the fear that the food will make the number go up, or that my body will change. It’s not logical, I know, especially at my age and with the doctor saying what he’s saying, but in the moment it feels terribly urgent.

I can’t just leave it alone and say, “Well, that’s normal,” or “It’s just a fluctuation.” It grabs hold of me.\n\nSo yes, after those eating episodes especially, I’m terrified I’ll gain weight from it. It’s not just a passing worry. It feels urgent, like I’ve lost control and need to undo the damage somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others tell you that you're too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I know that probably doesn’t make sense to other people, because my doctor has said straight out that I’m too thin. And I hear it, I really do. I’m not trying to be difficult. But when I look at myself, or when I get on the scale, it doesn’t feel true in my head.\n\nIf the number is up even half a pound, I get this awful panicky feeling, like I’ve suddenly let myself go. I feel ashamed, like I’ve done something wrong and I have to fix it right away. It’s not like I can calmly tell myself, “That’s nothing, that’s just normal.” I know other people might see it that way, but I don’t feel it that way. It feels much bigger than it is.\n\nDoctor: Is your main reason for making yourself vomit or using laxatives after eating too much because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… that is the main reason, I think. I wish I could say it was something else, or that it was just because I felt physically uncomfortable, but really it’s because I get frightened that the food is going to stay in me and turn into weight.\n\nThere are times after I’ve eaten too much where I’ll go straight to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. I don’t feel calm about it at all—it’s more like panic, like I have to get rid of it before it “counts,” if that makes sense. And I’ve taken laxatives for the same reason, because I’m scared of the food sitting inside me. It sounds awful saying it plainly, but yes… it’s mostly the fear of gaining weight that makes me do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the most important thing when it comes to how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, though I don’t like admitting that. It feels shameful, especially at my age, to say that a number on a scale or how I think my body looks can decide whether I feel all right with myself or not. But it does.\n\nEven when my doctor tells me I’m too thin, that doesn’t really settle it inside me. If I step on the scale and it’s up even half a pound, I can feel myself change right away. I get panicky, and ashamed, like I’ve let myself go somehow. It’s ridiculous, maybe, because half a pound shouldn’t mean anything, but to me it feels like I’ve failed, like I need to fix it immediately.\n\nSo yes, my weight and shape have too much power over how I feel about myself. More than anything else sometimes. It’s like I can’t judge myself fairly unless that number feels “safe,” and if it doesn’t, I feel awful about who I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your self-worth is largely defined by your weight and body shape?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s fair to say. I don’t like that it’s true, but it is. Even when my doctor tells me I’m too thin, it doesn’t seem to sink in the way it should. I can hear the words, but then I get on the scale and if it’s up even half a pound, everything in me just drops.\n\nI feel panicky, like something has gone terribly wrong, and ashamed too, like I’ve let myself go or lost control somehow. And then it’s not just about the number anymore — it feels like it says something about me, like I’ve failed and I have to fix it right away. I know half a pound probably sounds like nothing, but in my head it feels much bigger than that. So yes… my weight and shape do end up deciding how I feel about myself, more than I wish they did.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: It’s not every single time I eat too much, but… it has been happening regularly. I’ve been keeping food logs, and when I look back at them, for many months now, almost every week has at least one afternoon where I ate out of control and then tried to make up for it somehow.\n\nSometimes that meant making myself vomit. Other times I used laxatives, or I wouldn’t eat afterward, or I’d push myself with walking and chores until I felt like I’d done something to undo it. But vomiting is part of that pattern, yes. It’s usually after those afternoons when I feel like I’ve lost control, and then I panic and feel like I have to do something right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to make up for the extra food after one of these eating episodes by vomiting, using laxatives, or some other way?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, not every time in exactly the same way, but after those times when I feel I’ve eaten too much, I usually feel this awful panic, like I can’t just let it sit there in me.\n\nThere are times I go to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. I hate saying that out loud, but that is what happens. It’s like I tell myself I’ll feel calmer afterward, or that I’ve undone some of the damage, even though I know that probably sounds strange.\n\nAnd I’ve taken laxatives too, because I get scared the food will stay in me, or that I’ll gain from it. It’s not even always thought through clearly — it’s more like fear takes over and I’m trying to get rid of it somehow. So yes, I do try to make up for it that way.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you make yourself vomit or use laxatives after eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is. I wish I could say it was about feeling sick or uncomfortable after eating, but if I’m being honest, the fear of gaining weight is what really sets it off.\n\nEven when my doctor tells me I’m too thin, that doesn’t reassure me the way it probably should. I’ll hear it, and part of me knows I’m supposed to take that seriously, but then if I step on the scale and it’s up even half a pound, I just… I panic. It feels like I’ve let myself go, like I’ve lost control, and I feel ashamed, almost like I’ve done something wrong.\n\nAnd once that feeling starts, it’s very hard to sit with it. My mind goes straight to, “I have to fix this right now.” So yes, the vomiting or laxatives are tied to that fear — the fear that the food will make the number go up, or that my body will change. It’s not logical, I know, especially at my age and with the doctor saying what he’s saying, but in the moment it feels terribly urgent.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It’s not logical, I know, especially at my age and with the doctor saying what he’s saying, but in the moment it feels terribly urgent.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you usually try to make up for the extra food after one of these eating episodes most of the time?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 78 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Compensatory_Behavior_Purging\nmanifestation: \"There are times after I eat too much when I go to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up, and I’ve also taken laxatives because I’m scared the food will stay in me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… most of the time, I’d say I do try to make up for it somehow. Not always in the exact same way, but it’s hard for me to just leave it alone afterward.

There are times after I eat too much when I go to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. I don’t like admitting that, but that’s what happens. And other times I’ve taken laxatives, because I get scared the food is just going to stay in me, like I won’t be able to undo it or stop what it might do to my body.

It feels very urgent in the moment, like I have to do something right away. Even if later I feel ashamed or worn out by it, at the time it feels like I can’t calm down unless I try to get rid of it somehow.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brings me in is that I’m frightened of what happens when I start eating. It’s not just, you know, having a bit more than I meant to. It feels like something gets away from me. Once I start, my hands just keep going back for more, and I’m sitting there thinking, Tom, stop this now, you’ve had enough, just stop — but I don’t. It’s like the thought is there, plain as day, but it doesn’t seem to reach my hands somehow.\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds strange, especially at my age, but it’s been scaring me. I don’t feel in charge in those moments, and that’s the part that made me think I’d better talk to someone rather than just keep pretending I can handle it on my own.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I’m ashamed to say it, but there are times after I’ve eaten too much where I go straight to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. It’s not every single bite or anything like that, but when I feel I’ve gone too far, I get this awful panic that the food is just sitting there inside me, and I can’t settle.\n\nAnd I’ve taken laxatives too, for the same reason, really. I get scared the food will stay in me, and I tell myself I’m just trying to undo it somehow. I know that probably sounds terrible, especially saying it out loud, but yes… I have done that.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating too much happen?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to work that out, actually, because I knew if I came in here and just said “a lot,” that wouldn’t help much. I started writing it down in a little notebook—what I ate, when it happened, and what I did afterward—and looking back over it, it’s been going on for many months now.\n\nNearly every week there’s been at least one afternoon where it gets out of hand. Afternoons seem to be the worst for me. I’ll start eating and then it just keeps going, and afterward I’ll panic and try to make up for it somehow—sometimes by making myself sick, sometimes laxatives, sometimes not eating for a while after, or pushing myself too hard with walking around or doing chores.\n\nSo… not every day, no, but it’s not rare either. When I look at the logs, it’s almost every week, at least once, and it’s been that way for quite a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight as a result of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… very much. More than I like to admit, really. It sounds foolish when I say it out loud, because my doctor has told me I’m too thin, and I do hear that, I do understand the words. But it doesn’t feel that way in my head.\n\nIf I get on the scale and it’s up even half a pound, I feel this rush of panic, like I’ve done something wrong. I’ll stand there staring at the number as if it’s proof that I’ve let myself go, even if it’s such a tiny change that anyone else would probably say it means nothing. I feel ashamed, almost dirty with myself, and then I start thinking I have to fix it right away. I can’t just leave it alone and say, “Well, that’s normal,” or “It’s just a fluctuation.” It grabs hold of me.\n\nSo yes, after those eating episodes especially, I’m terrified I’ll gain weight from it. It’s not just a passing worry. It feels urgent, like I’ve lost control and need to undo the damage somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others tell you that you're too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I know that probably doesn’t make sense to other people, because my doctor has said straight out that I’m too thin. And I hear it, I really do. I’m not trying to be difficult. But when I look at myself, or when I get on the scale, it doesn’t feel true in my head.\n\nIf the number is up even half a pound, I get this awful panicky feeling, like I’ve suddenly let myself go. I feel ashamed, like I’ve done something wrong and I have to fix it right away. It’s not like I can calmly tell myself, “That’s nothing, that’s just normal.” I know other people might see it that way, but I don’t feel it that way. It feels much bigger than it is.\n\nDoctor: Is your main reason for making yourself vomit or using laxatives after eating too much because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… that is the main reason, I think. I wish I could say it was something else, or that it was just because I felt physically uncomfortable, but really it’s because I get frightened that the food is going to stay in me and turn into weight.\n\nThere are times after I’ve eaten too much where I’ll go straight to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. I don’t feel calm about it at all—it’s more like panic, like I have to get rid of it before it “counts,” if that makes sense. And I’ve taken laxatives for the same reason, because I’m scared of the food sitting inside me. It sounds awful saying it plainly, but yes… it’s mostly the fear of gaining weight that makes me do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the most important thing when it comes to how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, though I don’t like admitting that. It feels shameful, especially at my age, to say that a number on a scale or how I think my body looks can decide whether I feel all right with myself or not. But it does.\n\nEven when my doctor tells me I’m too thin, that doesn’t really settle it inside me. If I step on the scale and it’s up even half a pound, I can feel myself change right away. I get panicky, and ashamed, like I’ve let myself go somehow. It’s ridiculous, maybe, because half a pound shouldn’t mean anything, but to me it feels like I’ve failed, like I need to fix it immediately.\n\nSo yes, my weight and shape have too much power over how I feel about myself. More than anything else sometimes. It’s like I can’t judge myself fairly unless that number feels “safe,” and if it doesn’t, I feel awful about who I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your self-worth is largely defined by your weight and body shape?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s fair to say. I don’t like that it’s true, but it is. Even when my doctor tells me I’m too thin, it doesn’t seem to sink in the way it should. I can hear the words, but then I get on the scale and if it’s up even half a pound, everything in me just drops.\n\nI feel panicky, like something has gone terribly wrong, and ashamed too, like I’ve let myself go or lost control somehow. And then it’s not just about the number anymore — it feels like it says something about me, like I’ve failed and I have to fix it right away. I know half a pound probably sounds like nothing, but in my head it feels much bigger than that. So yes… my weight and shape do end up deciding how I feel about myself, more than I wish they did.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: It’s not every single time I eat too much, but… it has been happening regularly. I’ve been keeping food logs, and when I look back at them, for many months now, almost every week has at least one afternoon where I ate out of control and then tried to make up for it somehow.\n\nSometimes that meant making myself vomit. Other times I used laxatives, or I wouldn’t eat afterward, or I’d push myself with walking and chores until I felt like I’d done something to undo it. But vomiting is part of that pattern, yes. It’s usually after those afternoons when I feel like I’ve lost control, and then I panic and feel like I have to do something right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to make up for the extra food after one of these eating episodes by vomiting, using laxatives, or some other way?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, not every time in exactly the same way, but after those times when I feel I’ve eaten too much, I usually feel this awful panic, like I can’t just let it sit there in me.\n\nThere are times I go to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. I hate saying that out loud, but that is what happens. It’s like I tell myself I’ll feel calmer afterward, or that I’ve undone some of the damage, even though I know that probably sounds strange.\n\nAnd I’ve taken laxatives too, because I get scared the food will stay in me, or that I’ll gain from it. It’s not even always thought through clearly — it’s more like fear takes over and I’m trying to get rid of it somehow. So yes, I do try to make up for it that way.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you make yourself vomit or use laxatives after eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is. I wish I could say it was about feeling sick or uncomfortable after eating, but if I’m being honest, the fear of gaining weight is what really sets it off.\n\nEven when my doctor tells me I’m too thin, that doesn’t reassure me the way it probably should. I’ll hear it, and part of me knows I’m supposed to take that seriously, but then if I step on the scale and it’s up even half a pound, I just… I panic. It feels like I’ve let myself go, like I’ve lost control, and I feel ashamed, almost like I’ve done something wrong.\n\nAnd once that feeling starts, it’s very hard to sit with it. My mind goes straight to, “I have to fix this right now.” So yes, the vomiting or laxatives are tied to that fear — the fear that the food will make the number go up, or that my body will change. It’s not logical, I know, especially at my age and with the doctor saying what he’s saying, but in the moment it feels terribly urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to make up for the extra food after one of these eating episodes most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I’d say I do try to make up for it somehow. Not always in the exact same way, but it’s hard for me to just leave it alone afterward.\n\nThere are times after I eat too much when I go to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. I don’t like admitting that, but that’s what happens. or pushing myself with walking and chores.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I’ve pulled away from the garden group and family meals because I’m either planning around food, ashamed afterward, or worried someone will notice what I’m doing.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 years and 5 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and five months, since I stopped taking handyman jobs and stepped back from the garden workshops.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 78 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S050\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you usually try to make up for the extra food after one of these eating episodes by vomiting, using laxatives, or some other way?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, not every time in exactly the same way, but after those times when I feel I’ve eaten too much, I usually feel this awful panic, like I can’t just let it sit there in me.\n\nThere are times I go to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. I hate saying that out loud, but that is what happens. It’s like I tell myself I’ll feel calmer afterward, or that I’ve undone some of the damage, even though I know that probably sounds strange.\n\nAnd I’ve taken laxatives too, because I get scared the food will stay in me, or that I’ll gain from it. It’s not even always thought through clearly — it’s more like fear takes over and I’m trying to get rid of it somehow. So yes, I do try to make up for it that way.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you make yourself vomit or use laxatives after eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is. I wish I could say it was about feeling sick or uncomfortable after eating, but if I’m being honest, the fear of gaining weight is what really sets it off.\n\nEven when my doctor tells me I’m too thin, that doesn’t reassure me the way it probably should. I’ll hear it, and part of me knows I’m supposed to take that seriously, but then if I step on the scale and it’s up even half a pound, I just… I panic. It feels like I’ve let myself go, like I’ve lost control, and I feel ashamed, almost like I’ve done something wrong.\n\nAnd once that feeling starts, it’s very hard to sit with it. My mind goes straight to, “I have to fix this right now.” So yes, the vomiting or laxatives are tied to that fear — the fear that the food will make the number go up, or that my body will change. It’s not logical, I know, especially at my age and with the doctor saying what he’s saying, but in the moment it feels terribly urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to make up for the extra food after one of these eating episodes most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I’d say I do try to make up for it somehow. Not always in the exact same way, but it’s hard for me to just leave it alone afterward.\n\nThere are times after I eat too much when I go to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. I don’t like admitting that, but that’s what happens. And other times I’ve taken laxatives, because I get scared the food is just going to stay in me, like I won’t be able to undo it or stop what it might do to my body.\n\nIt feels very urgent in the moment, like I have to do something right away. Even if later I feel ashamed or worn out by it, at the time it feels like I can’t calm down unless I try to get rid of it somehow.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs the fear of gaining weight the only reason you make yourself vomit or use laxatives after eating too much?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I can say it’s the only reason, because sometimes it all gets mixed together in my head — feeling too full, feeling disgusted with myself, feeling like I’ve done something wrong. But the weight part is the thing underneath it, I think. That’s the part that makes it feel so urgent.

Even when my doctor says I’m too thin, it doesn’t really settle me. I hear him say it, and I can nod and understand it for a moment, but then if I get on the scale and it’s up even half a pound, I feel panicky. Ashamed, really. Like I’ve let myself go, like I’ve lost control somehow. And then my mind just goes straight to, “You have to fix this right now.”

So no, maybe it’s not the only feeling in the moment, but the fear of gaining weight is the main thing driving it. That’s the part I can’t seem to quiet down.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brings me in is that I’m frightened of what happens when I start eating. It’s not just, you know, having a bit more than I meant to. It feels like something gets away from me. Once I start, my hands just keep going back for more, and I’m sitting there thinking, Tom, stop this now, you’ve had enough, just stop — but I don’t. It’s like the thought is there, plain as day, but it doesn’t seem to reach my hands somehow.\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds strange, especially at my age, but it’s been scaring me. I don’t feel in charge in those moments, and that’s the part that made me think I’d better talk to someone rather than just keep pretending I can handle it on my own.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I’m ashamed to say it, but there are times after I’ve eaten too much where I go straight to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. It’s not every single bite or anything like that, but when I feel I’ve gone too far, I get this awful panic that the food is just sitting there inside me, and I can’t settle.\n\nAnd I’ve taken laxatives too, for the same reason, really. I get scared the food will stay in me, and I tell myself I’m just trying to undo it somehow. I know that probably sounds terrible, especially saying it out loud, but yes… I have done that.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating too much happen?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to work that out, actually, because I knew if I came in here and just said “a lot,” that wouldn’t help much. I started writing it down in a little notebook—what I ate, when it happened, and what I did afterward—and looking back over it, it’s been going on for many months now.\n\nNearly every week there’s been at least one afternoon where it gets out of hand. Afternoons seem to be the worst for me. I’ll start eating and then it just keeps going, and afterward I’ll panic and try to make up for it somehow—sometimes by making myself sick, sometimes laxatives, sometimes not eating for a while after, or pushing myself too hard with walking around or doing chores.\n\nSo… not every day, no, but it’s not rare either. When I look at the logs, it’s almost every week, at least once, and it’s been that way for quite a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight as a result of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… very much. More than I like to admit, really. It sounds foolish when I say it out loud, because my doctor has told me I’m too thin, and I do hear that, I do understand the words. But it doesn’t feel that way in my head.\n\nIf I get on the scale and it’s up even half a pound, I feel this rush of panic, like I’ve done something wrong. I’ll stand there staring at the number as if it’s proof that I’ve let myself go, even if it’s such a tiny change that anyone else would probably say it means nothing. I feel ashamed, almost dirty with myself, and then I start thinking I have to fix it right away. I can’t just leave it alone and say, “Well, that’s normal,” or “It’s just a fluctuation.” It grabs hold of me.\n\nSo yes, after those eating episodes especially, I’m terrified I’ll gain weight from it. It’s not just a passing worry. It feels urgent, like I’ve lost control and need to undo the damage somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others tell you that you're too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I know that probably doesn’t make sense to other people, because my doctor has said straight out that I’m too thin. And I hear it, I really do. I’m not trying to be difficult. But when I look at myself, or when I get on the scale, it doesn’t feel true in my head.\n\nIf the number is up even half a pound, I get this awful panicky feeling, like I’ve suddenly let myself go. I feel ashamed, like I’ve done something wrong and I have to fix it right away. It’s not like I can calmly tell myself, “That’s nothing, that’s just normal.” I know other people might see it that way, but I don’t feel it that way. It feels much bigger than it is.\n\nDoctor: Is your main reason for making yourself vomit or using laxatives after eating too much because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… that is the main reason, I think. I wish I could say it was something else, or that it was just because I felt physically uncomfortable, but really it’s because I get frightened that the food is going to stay in me and turn into weight.\n\nThere are times after I’ve eaten too much where I’ll go straight to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. I don’t feel calm about it at all—it’s more like panic, like I have to get rid of it before it “counts,” if that makes sense. And I’ve taken laxatives for the same reason, because I’m scared of the food sitting inside me. It sounds awful saying it plainly, but yes… it’s mostly the fear of gaining weight that makes me do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the most important thing when it comes to how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, though I don’t like admitting that. It feels shameful, especially at my age, to say that a number on a scale or how I think my body looks can decide whether I feel all right with myself or not. But it does.\n\nEven when my doctor tells me I’m too thin, that doesn’t really settle it inside me. If I step on the scale and it’s up even half a pound, I can feel myself change right away. I get panicky, and ashamed, like I’ve let myself go somehow. It’s ridiculous, maybe, because half a pound shouldn’t mean anything, but to me it feels like I’ve failed, like I need to fix it immediately.\n\nSo yes, my weight and shape have too much power over how I feel about myself. More than anything else sometimes. It’s like I can’t judge myself fairly unless that number feels “safe,” and if it doesn’t, I feel awful about who I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your self-worth is largely defined by your weight and body shape?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s fair to say. I don’t like that it’s true, but it is. Even when my doctor tells me I’m too thin, it doesn’t seem to sink in the way it should. I can hear the words, but then I get on the scale and if it’s up even half a pound, everything in me just drops.\n\nI feel panicky, like something has gone terribly wrong, and ashamed too, like I’ve let myself go or lost control somehow. And then it’s not just about the number anymore — it feels like it says something about me, like I’ve failed and I have to fix it right away. I know half a pound probably sounds like nothing, but in my head it feels much bigger than that. So yes… my weight and shape do end up deciding how I feel about myself, more than I wish they did.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: It’s not every single time I eat too much, but… it has been happening regularly. I’ve been keeping food logs, and when I look back at them, for many months now, almost every week has at least one afternoon where I ate out of control and then tried to make up for it somehow.\n\nSometimes that meant making myself vomit. Other times I used laxatives, or I wouldn’t eat afterward, or I’d push myself with walking and chores until I felt like I’d done something to undo it. But vomiting is part of that pattern, yes. It’s usually after those afternoons when I feel like I’ve lost control, and then I panic and feel like I have to do something right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to make up for the extra food after one of these eating episodes by vomiting, using laxatives, or some other way?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, not every time in exactly the same way, but after those times when I feel I’ve eaten too much, I usually feel this awful panic, like I can’t just let it sit there in me.\n\nThere are times I go to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. I hate saying that out loud, but that is what happens. It’s like I tell myself I’ll feel calmer afterward, or that I’ve undone some of the damage, even though I know that probably sounds strange.\n\nAnd I’ve taken laxatives too, because I get scared the food will stay in me, or that I’ll gain from it. It’s not even always thought through clearly — it’s more like fear takes over and I’m trying to get rid of it somehow. So yes, I do try to make up for it that way.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you make yourself vomit or use laxatives after eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is. I wish I could say it was about feeling sick or uncomfortable after eating, but if I’m being honest, the fear of gaining weight is what really sets it off.\n\nEven when my doctor tells me I’m too thin, that doesn’t reassure me the way it probably should. I’ll hear it, and part of me knows I’m supposed to take that seriously, but then if I step on the scale and it’s up even half a pound, I just… I panic. It feels like I’ve let myself go, like I’ve lost control, and I feel ashamed, almost like I’ve done something wrong.\n\nAnd once that feeling starts, it’s very hard to sit with it. And I’ve taken laxatives for the same reason, because I’m scared of the food sitting inside me. It sounds awful saying it plainly, but yes… it’s mostly the fear of gaining weight that makes me do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the most important thing when it comes to how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, though I don’t like admitting that. It feels shameful, especially at my age, to say that a number on a scale or how I think my body looks can decide whether I feel all right with myself or not. But it does.\n\nEven when my doctor tells me I’m too thin, that doesn’t really settle it inside me. If I step on the scale and it’s up even half a pound, I can feel myself change right away. I get panicky, and ashamed, like I’ve let myself go somehow. It’s ridiculous, maybe, because half a pound shouldn’t mean anything, but to me it feels like I’ve failed, like I need to fix it immediately.\n\nSo yes, my weight and shape have too much power over how I feel about myself. More than anything else sometimes. It’s like I can’t judge myself fairly unless that number feels “safe,” and if it doesn’t, I feel awful about who I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your self-worth is largely defined by your weight and body shape?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s fair to say. I don’t like that it’s true, but it is. Even when my doctor tells me I’m too thin, it doesn’t seem to sink in the way it should. I can hear the words, but then I get on the scale and if it’s up even half a pound, everything in me just drops.\n\nI feel panicky, like something has gone terribly wrong, and ashamed too, like I’ve let myself go or lost control somehow. And then it’s not just about the number anymore — it feels like it says something about me, like I’ve failed and I have to fix it right away. I know half a pound probably sounds like nothing, but in my head it feels much bigger than that. So yes… my weight and shape do end up deciding how I feel about myself, more than I wish they did.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: It’s not every single time I eat too much, but… it has been happening regularly. I’ve been keeping food logs, and when I look back at them, for many months now, almost every week has at least one afternoon where I ate out of control and then tried to make up for it somehow.\n\nSometimes that meant making myself vomit. Other times I used laxatives, or I wouldn’t eat afterward, or I’d push myself with walking and chores until I felt like I’d done something to undo it. But vomiting is part of that pattern, yes. It’s usually after those afternoons when I feel like I’ve lost control, and then I panic and feel like I have to do something right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to make up for the extra food after one of these eating episodes by vomiting, using laxatives, or some other way?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, not every time in exactly the same way, but after those times when I feel I’ve eaten too much, I usually feel this awful panic, like I can’t just let it sit there in me.\n\nThere are times I go to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. I hate saying that out loud, but that is what happens. It’s like I tell myself I’ll feel calmer afterward, or that I’ve undone some of the damage, even though I know that probably sounds strange.\n\nAnd I’ve taken laxatives too, because I get scared the food will stay in me, or that I’ll gain from it. It’s not even always thought through clearly — it’s more like fear takes over and I’m trying to get rid of it somehow. So yes, I do try to make up for it that way.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you make yourself vomit or use laxatives after eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is. I wish I could say it was about feeling sick or uncomfortable after eating, but if I’m being honest, the fear of gaining weight is what really sets it off.\n\nEven when my doctor tells me I’m too thin, that doesn’t reassure me the way it probably should. I’ll hear it, and part of me knows I’m supposed to take that seriously, but then if I step on the scale and it’s up even half a pound, I just… I panic. It feels like I’ve let myself go, like I’ve lost control, and I feel ashamed, almost like I’ve done something wrong.\n\nAnd once that feeling starts, it’s very hard to sit with it. My mind goes straight to, “I have to fix this right now.” So yes, the vomiting or laxatives are tied to that fear — the fear that the food will make the number go up, or that my body will change. It’s not logical, I know, especially at my age and with the doctor saying what he’s saying, but in the moment it feels terribly urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to make up for the extra food after one of these eating episodes most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I’d say I do try to make up for it somehow. Not always in the exact same way, but it’s hard for me to just leave it alone afterward.\n\nThere are times after I eat too much when I go to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. I don’t like admitting that, but that’s what happens. And other times I’ve taken laxatives, because I get scared the food is just going to stay in me, like I won’t be able to undo it or stop what it might do to my body.\n\nIt feels very urgent in the moment, like I have to do something right away. Even if later I feel ashamed or worn out by it, at the time it feels like I can’t calm down unless I try to get rid of it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight the only reason you make yourself vomit or use laxatives after eating too much?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say it’s the only reason, because sometimes it all gets mixed together in my head — feeling too full, feeling disgusted with myself, feeling like I’ve done something wrong. But the weight part is the thing underneath it, I think. That’s the part that makes it feel so urgent.\n\nEven when my doctor says I’m too thin, it doesn’t really settle me. I hear him say it, and I can nod and understand it for a moment, but then if I get on the scale and it’s up even half a pound, I feel panicky. Ashamed, really. Like I’ve let myself go, like I’ve lost control somehow. And then my mind just goes straight to, “You have to fix this right now.”\n\nSo no, maybe it’s not the only feeling in the moment, but the fear of gaining weight is the main thing driving it. That’s the part I can’t seem to quiet down.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s hard for me to give an exact number, because I don’t always write it down or anything, and honestly I try not to think about it too directly. But it does happen more than just once in a while.

There are times after I eat too much when I go to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. It’s usually when I’m scared the food is going to stay in me and I can’t calm myself down. Some months it feels like it happens several times, maybe more depending on how many of those eating episodes I’ve had. And when I don’t vomit, I’ve also taken laxatives for the same reason — that fear that the food is just sitting there and I need to do something about it.

I’m sorry, I know you asked how many times, and I’m not answering very neatly. I’d say… several times a month at least, but I’d have to really think to count it properly.
